After DoorDash’s Instacart bid, here’s the case for and against consolidation in commerce media

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The “C” word (consolidation) might soon be coming for commerce media.

At least that was how media buyers and industry analysts reacted to last week’s news about DoorDash’s plans to acquire Instacart.

“We’ve seen this play out before in search, social, and streaming. Markets tend to consolidate around a few major players, and commerce media is heading in the same direction,” said Bill Wise, CEO and co-founder of Mediaocean, an ad tech company.

Should the deal, which is valued at approximately $17 billion, go through, it pairs DoorDash’s delivery network of 993 million total orders, per DoorDash’s Q3 earnings and Instacart’s grocery and fulfillment technology (as well as its 91.4 million orders, per Instacart’s Q3 earnings).

Here’s the case for and against consolidation in commerce media.

Perhaps the biggest case in favor of consolidation in commerce media is that the playbook already exists in ad tech. For example, Gopuff announced plans in September to leverage Instacart’s white-label retail media platform Carrot Ads. Both Gopuff and Instacart have separate commerce media networks.

“You could kind of argue data consolidation is also happening across partners who are working together to make their joint offerings more compelling,” said Briana Finelli, vp of brand and commerce media at Goodway Group.

As for the DoorDash-Instacart deal specifically, the upsides are a broader audience, more powerful closed-loop reporting capabilities, combined tech offerings and a more simplified media buying experience (buying with a single combined platform as opposed to across two individuals).

“Add in a scaled ads platform featuring sponsored products, search, offsite capabilities, social and search and what don’t you have,” said Elizabeth Marsten, vp of commerce innovation and growth at Tinuiti.

By 2028, eMarketer forecasts Amazon’s retail media revenues will rake in at least $75 billion. Advertisers are spending more on commerce and retail media, but are looking for simplicity in a fragmented marketplace.

“The opportunity is there to build a much more competitive retail media offering, but execution and speed will be critical,” said Nik Hengel, vp of analytics at New Engen digital marketing agency.

While the idea of bigger, farther reaching commerce media platforms sounds appetizing, consolidation doesn’t come without its considerations.

For starters, a shinier offering could come with a premium price tag. Or as Chief Media CEO Scott Paternoster puts it, “The cons could be everything that everyone thinks about consolidation, with price pressures and whatnot.”

There’s also the painful process of combining two separate companies’ culture, tech stacks, and staff that could disrupt how advertisers buy media. Already, advertisers have started to question what the DoorDash-Instacart merger means for capabilities and measurement.

Expect other questions around audience duplication, said Greg Carlucci, senior director analyst at Gartner.

While consolidation has its perks, one particular consideration is how much control advertisers have in negotiating with a behemoth. Just look at the relationship between indie agencies and major demand side platforms.

“That’s why independent infrastructure matters even more as the market consolidates,” said Wise. He added, “Advertisers need a consistent way to buy across platforms, with the right context, without being locked into any one ecosystem.”

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

Agencies find AI overuse is leading to brief bloat

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of Digiday’s coverage of its Digiday Media Buying Summit. More from the series →

The advertising world rushed to embrace generative AI. But now that agencies and advertisers have come to rely on the tech to keep up, they’re finding themselves vulnerable to its toxic byproducts.

In particular, agency leaders are finding the over-use of AI tools to write advertising briefs risks undermining the productivity and the day-to-day sanity of marketing staffers.

According to M&C Saatchi’s North American CEO, Nadja Bellan-White, over-reliance on AI for brief writing is approaching “a tipping point… It’s a little bit ridiculous.” Speaking during last week’s Digiday Media Buying Summit, she estimated that 85% of the briefs sent to the agency by clients were written using AI tools.

The impact? Corporate documents that run into the hundreds of pages, replete with marketing jargon but little actual vision. “How many many times can you say ‘framework’ in one sentence? How many times can you try ‘economies of scale’? How many times can you tell me I need this to ‘tie back to commercial objectives’?”

Brief writing has become one of the most common applications for AI tech in marketing. More than half (56.4%) of marketers and agencies now use it to build out strategy, media or campaign creative briefs, according to a survey conducted by Basis Technologies between July and August.

Media agencies use agents to suggest audience segments, run analyses of competitors’ media plans or suggest search keywords to bid on. Creative shops and influencer agencies use AI to skip through reams of consumer research, brand guidelines and economic trends, or to find ways of trumping received marketing wisdom. In-house teams at high-spending brands use generative tools to spin up strategy approaches. Even publishers now use agentic tools that respond to client briefs with AI-generated insights.

Staffers will point to the hours clawed back or to the insights discovered from a broader or deeper reading of research. Behind closed doors, however, they kvetch about the cost.

“I’ve seen some of the worst briefs I’ve ever seen in my career recently, and it’s because they’re so disjointed and there’s no through-line on what… the marketing goal is,” said one agency executive, speaking under Chatham House rules at a townhall session at DMBS.

“AI is making it worse. It’s pontificating on a sentence structure that makes [a brief] 10 pages long,” said another.

Complaints about the proliferation of memos or emails, or indeed badly written campaign briefs, aren’t new. At the start of the decade, industry veterans will recall there was even a cross-industry initiative to encourage better brief writing. But just as it’s sped up the production of digital creative assets, Claude and ChatGPT tools have enabled bad writing to spread like knotweed. As another agency exec put it: “There’s a lack of critical thinking.”

Industry leaders are beginning to acknowledge those problems in public. “[AI] can be a good sparring partner,” said Bellan-White. “But at the end of the day, you need to be the one to make the decision. You understand the client’s briefs. You understand the market context. You’ve got to really think about what it is that’s recommended.”

It’s not hard to see how AI has become a crutch. Good briefs take time to craft, time that clients are loath to grant their agencies, or to use themselves, for something so far removed from the end product. Little surprise, then, that staffers turn to a solution that does the work for them like a white-collar version of the shoemaker’s elves.

In the Brothers Grimm tale, however, the shoemaker eventually retakes control of production. But advertising staffers are long-term practitioners of what academics call “cognitive offloading,” the delegation of knowledge work to a tool or machine.

A growing body of research suggests that such over-reliance on AI tools literally reduces the cognitive abilities of users. One 2025 MIT Media Lab study, for example, measured the brainwaves of students writing essays without the aid of technology, compared with those writing with the assistance of search engines or ChatGPT. The AI-assisted group showed a 55% reduction in brainwave activity.

Whatever the name, the signs are the same. A profusion of em-dashes, paragraph upon paragraph of empty, over-long sentences, the phrase “it’s not X, it’s Y.”

Cognitive offloading isn’t just an abstract (or aesthetic) problem for ad agency leaders, some of whom are recognizing that some marketing staffers have gone from using AI as an aid to relying upon it to prop them up. Bloated documents steal time from colleagues or clients, while many users’ loose attitude to the statistics AI tools conjure up demand for a closer-than usual supervision by fact-checkers. According to a GWI survey of 150 British marketers published last month, 29% admitted inaccurate or misleading insights had cost their companies financially.

“They have exported all of their thinking to AI,” said Tony Russo, vp, innovation and strategy at indie agency Mile Marker, during another DMBS session. “It’s a judgment problem.”

If there is a solution, it’s an offline one. Christina Riccitelli, chief innovation and analytics officer at media agency OpAD, said managers should intervene if they found junior staff leaning too hard on AI. “You have to reel them back in,” she said.

Bellan-White recommended more drastic action. “Turn off your laptop. Just literally shut it down. Turn off your phones,” she said. “I want you to look me in the eye and actually have a conversation. Do you know how uncomfortable that is for people today? Because if you actually have to have a conversation, that means you actually have to know what you’re talking about.”

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

The DoorDash-Instacart deal could accelerate commerce media consolidation, promising scale but risking higher prices and lock-in.

In graphic detail: Six thousand brands later, ChatGPT ads still have a conversion problem

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

ChatGPT now handles roughly a fifth of the search queries Google does. Its ads business is worth considerably less. Nobody should be surprised by that. OpenAI only started selling ads this year, the format they’re growing into is still taking shape and the company has been tiptoeing around how much advertising to put in front of users who came for users. Advertising is, in other words, lagging usage. OpenAI will want that to change quickly and advertisers are already testing how fast it can.

There’s room for OpenAI to increase the reach of its ad business within markets where it already operates. According to data from Similarweb and Graphite, the U.K. has the highest proportion of ad-eligible ChatGPT users seeing ads (45.7%), followed by the U.S. (43.4%) and Australia (43.3%).

But the data shows fewer than half of eligible users, even in OpenAI’s most established ad markets, are currently being reached by ads. In Mexico, for example, that figure falls to just 27.8%. The differences could reflect how far along each market is in the advertising rollout, or how much of its eligible audience OpenAI is currently monetizing.

The mix of advertisers spending on ChatGPT is also broadening beyond retail. According to data from Sensor Tower, shopping accounted for 38% of U.S. ad spend on the platform in April, but that share fell to 20% by September.

Over the same period, financial services increased its share of spend on ChatGPT from 2% to 13%, while software grew from 13% to 18%, and travel and tourism increased from 5% to 9%. This suggests that ChatGPT is attracting demand from a wider range of advertisers, rather than relying on a narrow range of verticals looking to reach consumers in shopping-related moments.

This has certainly broadened since the ad pilot launched in February. While Sensor Tower only started tracking ad spend in April 2026, at launch, the company estimates that around 42% of ads on ChatGPT were from advertisers in the shopping, followed by jobs & education (10%), food & dining (9%), software (8%), media and entertainment (8%) and travel (7%).

The number of brands advertising on ChatGPT is growing too. Data from Adthena shows that it tracked 1,082 distinct brands running ads on the platform, across the U.S., Australia, the U.K. and the EU in June. That number rose to 3,741 in July and 6,333 in August. And while the data for September only goes as far as Sept. 20, that full month is expected to reach around 6,830, based on Adthena’s data and projections.

It points to a growing pool of brands advertising on ChatGPT as the platform expands its markets and advertising capabilities.

New Street Research models the number of commercial chats on ChatGPT to grow from 45.9 billion this year, to nearly 380 billion by 2030, as the proportion of total chats carrying ads increases from 20% to 28% during the same period.

But the volume of ad opportunities is only part of the equation. The way those conversations are monetized will also matter. OpenAI recently said CPC and outcome-optimized bidding account for the majority of its ad campaigns, a higher proportion than New Street had previously assumed in its own model.

That could be positive for revenue, given CPC ads typically generate more per-ad event than impression-based pricing. However, New Street cautions that monetization may currently be weaker than it had originally modelled, suggesting its estimated CPC of around $5 could be too high.

That said, there is a question over whether ChatGPT ads are currently delivering enough value to advertisers to support the kind of revenue growth OpenAI is targeting. Research from a working paper under review by Maximilian Kaiser and Christian Schulze, based on data from ecommerce websites, found that paid ChatGPT visits generate $0.89 in revenue per session for U.S. advertisers, compared with $2.45 for paid Google Search visits.

The difference was also reflected in conversion rates: 0.37% for paid ChatGPT traffic, compared with 1.17% for Google. The findings suggest that while advertisers are increasingly testing the platform, OpenAI still has work to do to demonstrate that its ads can compete with established performance channels.

Those performance questions are compounded by ongoing measurement issues, according to Accuracast’s group CEO Farhad Divecha, who said the latest partner integrations — Magnite and Pubmatic — have not necessarily addressed the underlying tracking issues.

“We continue to see discrepancies there,” he said. “Some are teething problems because it’s a new system that has to be set up on advertiser systems, but some problems are down to attribution being messed up.”

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

Alabama Barker revealed during the Nov. 5 episode of The Kardashians how she and brother Landon Barker were able to make up after a period of not speaking.

Alabama Barker’s rift with Landon Barker turned out to be just a small thing.

After the 20-year-old revealed she and her older brother stopped speaking for weeks following a disagreement, there’s still true care between the two naturally.

"You know what? It was really sibling-coded,” Alabama told stepmom Kourtney Kardashian during the Nov. 5 episode of The Kardashians about the pair making up. “He looked at me—and then that's how we started communicating again."

As she told Kourtney, “We never established—we never spoke about it.”

“I found something out, and I told my father,” she explained. “And [Landon] didn’t like that,” noting that at the time, they were estranged for “going on four weeks.”

Guide: How To Unlock The Hall Of Fallen Foes In Zelda: Ocarina Of Time

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Where to find the Switch 2 remake's big new feature

The Hall of Fallen Foes is a new addition to Zelda: Ocarina of Time's Switch 2 remake.

The feature is both a museum cataloguing all the enemies you've encountered so far, and a way to rematch bosses for an extra challenge.

Here's how to unlock the Hall of Fallen Foes in Zelda: Ocarina of Time, and how to leave when you're done wiping the floor with the returning adversaries within.

On this page: How To Unlock The Hall Of Fallen Foes In Zelda: Ocarina Of Time

The Hall of Fallen Foes unlocks when you become Adult Link, and have the ability to turn back into Child Link via the Temple of Time once you have completing the Forest Temple dungeon.

From there, approach Kakariko Village from Hyrule Field as Child Link, where Kaepora Gaebora will halt you just before you enter and explain how accessing the Hall of Fallen Foes works.

Kaepora Gaebora explains that you can access the Hall of Fallen Foes by sleeping in a bed. There are multiple spots you can do this across the game, but the closest location from here (which Kaepora Gaebora will point you toward) is the Grand Lodge in the south of Kakariko Village.

Whether it's here or via resting place, you know you can access the Hall of Fallen Foes when a bed is glowing green.

Approach, choose to sleep and dream, and you'll awaken in the Hall of Fallen Foes. Here you can find out details of all the enemies you have defeated on your quest so far, with bosses on the outer edges for you to battle once more in a boss rush-style mode.

Once done, talk to Kaepora Gaebora to leave. A useful note: any items or resources you use in the Hall of Fallen Foes will be replenished when you return to the main adventure, so feel free to go wild with your tactics during a boss rematch.

Looking for more help with Zelda: Ocarina of Time? See our walkthrough hub for where to go next and a compendium of all our guides - which includes Heart Pieces, Gold Skulltulas, how to get Horse Epona, and what amiibo unlocks do.

Matthew - an award-winning UK-based writer and editor specialising in guides and service articles - joined the Nintendo Life team in May 2026.

Guide: Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Walkthrough (Switch 2 Remake): Guide To The Main Quest, Side Quests & Collectables

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Zelda: Ocarina of Time is a dense adventure with many secrets to find, and while the critical path is fairly linear, it's rarely straightforward - asking you to pick up select items and upgrades before you can start exploring its many dungeons.

That's why a Zelda: Ocarina of Time walkthrough comes in handy. Though the Switch 2 version follows the same beats as the N64 original, we'd estimate around 10% of the game's puzzles and locations have been changed or updated - just enough to throw off those very familiar with the 1998 version.

This page features both an abridged and thorough walkthrough though the critical path, plus a repository of our other Zelda: Ocarina of Time guides, from optional side-quests to items and collectables.

On this page: Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Walkthrough (Switch 2 Remake): Guide To The Main Quest, Side Quests & Collectables

If you want brief directions and as few spoilers as possible in terms of item unlocks and progression, then use the following abridged walkthrough to take you through the game. If you want extra specifics - including useful optional extras as you go - then check the following section.

For a more detailed walkthrough of the critical path, this section can explain specific steps on how to progress. If you even more detailed step-by-step guides to the game's many dungeons, then follow the links to dedicated pages on each.

Before you can visit the Great Deku Tree, you must find a Sword and Shield, otherwise Mido won’t let you through. You can find the Kokori Sword in the Forest Training Center to the south-west (crawl through the vent to get there, then find it in the chest above), and the Deku Shield from the shop in the north-east by collecting Rupees found scattered around the village.

Visit the Deku Tree, complete the Great Deku Tree dungeon, and get the Kokiri’s Emerald key item and an instruction to head to Hyrule Castle. Now leave Kokiri Forest to the west to receive an Ocarina in a cutscene before leaving to Hyrule Field.

Head directly north along the path until you reach the castle drawbridge. The area inside is the Market - there’s lots of optional activities here, and when ready, proceed to Hyrule Castle, where after getting the Weird Egg from Maron, you must make your way through the grounds to meet Zelda.

You'll now have Zelda’s Letter and learnt Zelda’s Lullaby on the Ocarina. The critical path is to Death Mountain via Kakariko Village, though you should visit the Lost Woods first (for Saria's Song, which you'll need shortly) and ideally, an optional visit to Lon Lon Ranch (for a Bottle and the first step in how to get Epona).

When ready, go east from Hyrule Field’s drawbridge and up the steps to Kakariko Village.

After finding all Cucco locations for a Bottle and visiting the Graveyard for the Sun’s Song (both optional), show Zelda’s Letter to the guard at the north exit. He gives you an optional errand - returning to the Hyrule Castle Market for a "proper shield" (the Hylian Shield) and also to start the Happy Mask Shop quest.

The aim is to reach Goron City. Hug the right most wall: this will take you up and round the corner, then back on yourself up the cliff. The path will now split: take the left branch to Goron City.

Inside Goron City, head to the lowest level, where at the north side is a closest door. Play Zelda’s Lullaby here to open it. Inside is Darunia. Talk to him, then play Saria’s Song (which you can get from the Lost Woods). This will see you unlock the Goron’s Bracelet, allowing you to use Bomb Flowers.

Now leave Goron City the way you came (the top entrance), then go directly right to see a Bomb Flower near a fence. Now you have the Goron's Bracelet, throw it over and you’ll bomb the entrance to Dodongo Cavern, allowing you to enter.

Once you have completed the Dodongo Cavern dungeon and picked Bombs and the Goron’s Ruby (the Spiritual Stone of Fire), feel free to scale Death Mountain for a Great Fairy location (unlocking Din's Fire) before heading off to Zora Domain.

Zora River - which leads to Zora Domain - is just east of Kakariko Village and Death Mountain on Hyrule Field.

Reach the end - the way there is fairly linear - and access Zora Domain by playing Zelda's Lullaby at the waterfall.

Inside, you must find the whereabouts of Princess Ruto by getting the Silver Scale in a mini-game at the top of the waterfall, then by visiting Lake Hylia via the underwater shortcut for a Bottle with a letter inside. (Our dedicated Zora Domain walkthrough features more specific directions for all these, by the way!)

Speak to King Zora, Bottle up a fish found in Zora Domain, then give it to the giant fish in Zora Fountain to access Lord Jabu-Jabu's Belly dungeon.

Once the dungeon is complete and the Spiritual Stone of Water is in your possession, head to Hyrule Castle. After the cutscene, dive in the moat and collect the Ocarina of Time, where you’ll then learn the Song of Time.

Head to the Temple of Time which branches off from Hyrule Castle Market. Inside, stand in front of the inscription at the far end, and play the Song of Time. Now go into the next room and retrieve the Master Sword.

After the cutscene, you’ll become Adult Link, and are told to head to Kakariko Village. Do so, then head to the Graveyard. On the left row of graves is a tombstone with flowers in front; push the stone and head down the hole. Now follow Dampe through the maze and at the end, you’ll receive the Hookshot.

To leave, play the Song of Time on the blocks, to emerge out in the Windmill. Use this opportunity to get the Heart Piece opposite, then speak to the man to learn the Song of Storms - you’ll need this later.

With the Hookshot in hand, you can explore more of Hyrule, but when ready, head to Forest Temple: it can be found at the end of the Lost Woods, and inside the dungeon, you'll unlock the Bow and be rewarded with the Forest Medallion story item.

Complete the Forest Temple dungeon to unlock a Sage. At this point onward, upon returning to the Temple of Time, you can time travel back into Young Link by putting the Master Sword the pedestal. Also, to help, Sheik will teach you The Prelude of Light, which allows you to teleport back to the Temple of Time - a pretty handy shortcut.

As Adult Link, make your way to Death Mountain (it’s worth going into the Shooting Gallery in Kakariko Village now you have the Bow, which rewards you the Big Quiver to hold 40 Rupees as you go) then head into Goron City.

On the next level down is a Goron rolling around, and your aim is to detonate a Bomb near him so he stops. Once successful, the Goron will reveal their name is Link, son of Darunia, and will explain the Goron's plight.

Exhaust the dialogue options to receive the Goron Tunic - a red garb which allows you to withstand high temperatures - and directions to the Fire Temple, which is by heading to Darunia’s room (located on the bottom level at the back) and then by pulling the statue to reveal a route to the Death Mountain Crater.

Once in, go over the drawbridge using the Hookshot to meet Sheik, who will teach you Bolero of Fire. Now head through the left doorway, drop down and follow the passage to reach the dungeon, unlocking the Megaton Hammer and the Fire Medallion along the way.

Head to the Ice Cavern, which is located in Zora Domain (or specifically, in Zora Fountain where you encountered Lord Jabu-Jabu as a child). Doing so will see you unlock the Iron Boots; make sure you grab a Blue Fire before leaving, and return to Zora Domain and use the Blue Fire on King Zora to free him and receive a Blue Tunic, which allows you to breathe underwater for an unlimited amount of time.

With both the Iron Boots and Blue Tunic in hand, we have everything we need to take on the Water Temple. Play the Serenade of Water to be teleported there when ready. Completing this will see you receive the Longshot - a Hookshot upgrade - and the Water Medallion.

Before we move onto the next temple, on this island is a plaque in front of you which hints at a puzzle involving the morning sun. If you are reading this as soon as the Water Temple has wrapped, the sun should still be rising: aim for the halo with your Bow and fire an Arrow, which will see it transform into a Fire Arrow on the platform ahead.

When you’re ready, head to Kakariko Village as Adult Link. When you arrive, a cutscene plays showing the place on fire, Sheik appearing, and a spirit emerges from the well. You’re then taught the Nocturne of Shadow, allowing you to travel to the Shadow Temple - though there’s a place to visit you need to visit first - the Kakariko well.

As it's currently blocked off as an adult, so you need to return as a child. If you haven’t already, learn Song of Storms from the Guru-Guru inside the windmill, then return to the windmill as Young Link and play Song of Storms back to him (now you have the Nocturne of Shadow, the return journey will be a lot quicker).

Doing so will cause the windmill to spin out of control and lower the water in the well. Drop down and complete the Bottom of the Well mini-dungeon to get the Lens of Truth.

If you haven’t already, you also need Din’s Fire. This is acquired by visiting the Great Fairy location at the top of Death Mountain. Now you have Din’s Fire and the Lens of Truth, play Nocturne of Shadow, then use Din’s Fire in the centre of the torches to gain access to the Shadow Temple, where you’ll get the Hover Boots along the way, and another Medallion.

With that done, it’s time to head to Gerudo Valley. It’s found in the west of Hyrule Field, and you need either the Longshot or Epona to make it over the bridge at the start.

Complete the Gerudo’s Fortress stealth section - essentially let yourself get captured, then find the four carpenters so you can explore freely - then make your way across the Mirage Desert's trials (you need the Longshot and the Lens of Truth) to reach the Evil Desert Colossus area and the Spirit Temple. This unlocks two new items - the Silver Gauntlets and the Mirror Shield - and another Medallion.

The Gerudo Valley area also features two optional extras - another Great Fairy spell (Bomb the wall on the north wall of the Evil Desert Colossus) and within Gerudo’s Fortress, the Gerudo Training Ground (a mini-dungeon which grants the Ice Arrow.

Assuming you have now awakened every Sage and received every Medallion, it’s now time to head to the Temple of Time and start the final stretch of the game.

A cutscene will play and you’ll receive the Light Arrow, and an instruction to head to Ganon’s Castle.

Approach the lava moat around the castle to form a bridge with the help of the Sages. The dungeon within is composed of six ‘routes’ from a central hub; though you can tackle them in broadly any order, go for the Shadow Barrier to unlock the Golden Gauntlets first, which is required for completing every puzzle.

Once that - and a brief trip to the Great Fairy just outside the castle beyond the bridge for a useful defensive upgrade - it’s time to scale the tower, or to halt your progress so you can mop up any remaining side-quests and collectables (see the end of this guide for a list) before the finale.

The tower within Ganondorf’s Castle is essentially a short gauntlet of battles until you reach Ganon himself at the top, and from there, it’s a linear stretch until the end credits. Good luck!

Here is a list of side quests, items and collectables you can find. Note this is in-progress, and will become more comprehensive in the days following launch:

Matthew - an award-winning UK-based writer and editor specialising in guides and service articles - joined the Nintendo Life team in May 2026.

Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 5, 2026

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The Moon is now approaching the end of its current lunar cycle, with less than 20% of its surface illuminated. This puts it near the end of the Waning Crescent phase, when just a thin sliver of the Moon remains visible before the New Moon.

As of Friday, Nov. 5, NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the Waning Crescent phase, with 16% of its surface visible.

Tonight, without the use of visual aids you should be able to spot the Aristarchus Plateau and that's all. With binoculars or a telescope, you'll be able to see this and the Grimaldi Basin.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

It takes the Moon around 29.5 days to pass through a complete lunar cycle. As it travels around Earth, the angle at which sunlight reaches its surface changes what we can see from here, producing the different phases, from the New Moon through to the Full Moon and back again. The cycle then starts over, as explained by NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

You can find bylines in publications including Fit&Well, Metro, and Coach magazine, usually covering deals on everything from earbuds to TVs, or guides on how to beat your half marathon time.

Lois also holds a Master's degree in Digital Journalism from Strathclyde University and obtained a Master of Arts in English Literature at the University of Aberdeen.

Hurdle hints and answers for November 5, 2026

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

Nintendo’s designers strove for ‘elegance’ when remaking Ocarina of Time

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Producers Eiji Aonuma and Hidemaro Fujibayashi talk about bringing the first 3D Zelda to a whole new audience.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Producers Eiji Aonuma and Hidemaro Fujibayashi talk about bringing the first 3D Zelda to a whole new audience.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The Ocarina of Time remake on the Switch 2 smashes together the past and present. The game retains much of the original N64 experience while updating it with modern graphics and gameplay that make it feel contemporary. But the mingling of time periods also extends to the development team. While Ocarina’s remake was led by producers Eiji Aonuma (a director on the original game who is now the franchise’s steward at Nintendo) and Hidemaro Fujibayashi (best known for directing Breath of the Wild and its sequel), the team also featured a number of younger developers, including some who had never played the original. According to Fujibayashi, those new voices provided some much-needed perspective that shaped the remake.

“What really impressed me was the fact that they really embraced the theme of what not to change from the original,” Fujibayashi tells me ahead of Ocarina of Time’s launch when explaining the influence of the younger team members, who approached the project “very carefully.” He adds that “I feel confident that they weren’t necessarily pursuing something new for the sake of novelty when it might be unneeded and clutter the experience. They’re looking for elegance. Only what is needed, only what was important in the original, and how to carry that forward as their focus. And I think that’s a perspective they’ll be able to bring to future projects as well.”

Much has changed since Ocarina of Time first debuted on the Nintendo 64 in 1998, and Aonuma has had the unique perspective of working on both iterations of the game. For him, the most obvious difference from then to now is the scope of the production. “We used to have maybe just one person designing the backgrounds, and that’s something that you couldn’t do nowadays,” he says. “A lot of the process is not so different compared to the N64 days, but the scale [is different]: There’s a lot more time involved, a lot more people involved, and because of that it can be harder to manage these teams.”

What has also changed is the perspective of players. The original Ocarina of Time was Nintendo’s first stab at bringing The Legend of Zelda into three dimensions, and there are elements that can feel dated today; you can’t control the camera, for instance, or jump whenever you want. This feeling is only exacerbated by the fact that the most recent Zelda games have put a strong emphasis on player freedom. After building your own flying machine in Tears of the Kingdom, going back to the original Ocarina can feel restrictive. The team made a handful of changes, like giving players complete camera control and the ability to jump anywhere, which were designed to provide connective tissue between the remake and its present-day contemporaries.

“When you’re developing a game like this with the free jump, we have to adjust the world to compensate for that freedom of the player,” Aonuma says. “I wasn’t quite sure about doing this in this title, but as soon as I felt it I thought, ‘Okay, this is a really good way to give players another way to approach a goal in the game.’ It adds just a little bit more freedom compared to the original Ocarina of Time. For me, bringing forward a little more freedom in this title is helpful because it carries something forward from Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom. Having that connection to the recent titles I think was really helpful.”

The same could be said for the game’s overhauled graphics, which blend realism and fantasy art into a visual style that feels distinct from other Zelda adventures. Aonuma says the goal was to “bring out details of the world that people weren’t able to see in the original Ocarina of Time.” He adds that this was “something that we wanted to do in the original, but we didn’t have the technical compatibility at the time in terms of hardware output.”

That said, the core gameplay of Ocarina still holds up remarkably well, particularly compared to other early 3D games. Fujibayashi was working at Capcom when the original launched in 1998, but he distinctly remembers anticipating how Nintendo would approach solving many of the problems that plagued early attempts at big 3D worlds. One of the most impactful features in Ocarina is something called Z-targeting, which lets the player lock on to an enemy, making combat much more intuitive. “The solutions are something that still stick with me as a foundation for when I create 3D games,” Fujibayashi says.

For Aonuma, remaking Ocarina of Time was also an opportunity to address some issues he had with the original version of the game. For instance, one of the most infamous parts of the N64 Ocarina was Navi, a little fairy who serves as a guide for Link during his adventures. The problem was, in the process of trying to help the player, she could become overbearing and, in turn, something of a nuisance. While Navi is still ever-present in the remake, her chatter has been toned down quite a bit.

“One of the things that we were thinking about in [the N64] development process is: How do we lead the player to the end of this adventure?” Aonuma recalls. “Can they follow along with the story? Something that we used was the Navi hints, and some of these were added just because we felt in that moment a palpable need for the player to have a little extra information. But it’s something that’s a hard balance to hit. You don’t want to give an answer outright to a player, you want a little bit of an information gap so that they have an opportunity to think for themselves and make that stretch. The more hints you add, the more Navi would talk, and that would change the player experience. [In the remake] we were looking for ways to make all of that more comfortable and more streamlined. And so using in-game text to chronicle your adventures up to that point, so people could remind themselves what they’ve done on the quest so far, helps us get people to the end goal.”

But perhaps the biggest thing to address was the Water Temple, widely regarded as the most challenging dungeon in the original game. Even Fujibayashi tells me that he remembers it being “quite difficult” when he first played it. On the N64, players had to contend with an awkward camera and were forced to constantly open up a menu to use important items. But much of that has been modernized in the remake, making the dungeon less arduous, as players can now better see and navigate the underwater dungeon. “I think when people make comparisons from their memory now to the original version of the Water Temple they’re going to feel quite a difference,” Aonuma explains after I tell him I found the new version of the dungeon much easier. “So this is really nice to hear. As soon as you said that I thought, ‘We did it.’”

Aonuma also felt it was important to keep Ocarina’s more traditional style of Zelda gameplay alive as the franchise celebrates its 40th anniversary this year. Breath of the Wild showed what could happen when you turned Hyrule into a true open world, Echoes of Wisdom took classic top-down gameplay and mixed it with an inventive new style of puzzle, and Ocarina offers something more straightforward. He believes that having all of those very different iterations on Zelda is part of the reason the franchise has had such staying power. “You need different approaches,” he says. And it’s those new developers, many of whom are fresh to Zelda, that he hopes will keep that philosophy going.

“We’ve had opportunities to add to the playbook of Zelda, if you will, and the language of development,” he explains. “You get to see the progression of these ideas across the franchise. And now, working with younger staff on this remake, we’re having the opportunity to bring in additional new ideas. I really look forward to the future and what’s possible coming from the new staff.”

Latin America EV Sales Report: With Over 200,000 Units Sold in Q3, EVs Now Stand at 12% Market Share!

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Through 2026, EVs have gained significant momentum all over the world (safe for the US), and Latin America has been no exception. Sales grew by almost 140% in the third quarter, and clock in at a very healthy 120% growth through 2026, bringing the yearly total to more than half a million units. If this trend continues, it’s all but certain we will surpass 700,000 units by the end of the year, and perhaps even get to 800,000.

Another interesting bit of trivia is that sales reached 220,000 units in the third quarter, not too far behind the USA’s 266,000. In theory, this means it should be possible for LatAm to surpass total US EV sales as early as Q1 2027 … though, I would be much happier if the USA’s market starts growing strongly again and thus Latin America cannot get ahead.

Now, to be fair, the US is much more BEV-heavy (242,000 units sold, vs 127,000 in LatAm), whereas Latin America prefers PHEVs to a higher degree (93,000 units sold vs 44,000 in the US), but, still, these are impressive results for a region overall much poorer than the US, and with a vehicle market around a third of the size. For over a decade, it was the richest countries in the world that led EV adoption, but — as many of us predicted on this very site — that has now changed.

As mentioned earlier, total EV sales reached 220,000 units, surpassing for the first time the 200,000 mark and growing a healthy 140% YoY, and a strong 17% from Q2. BEVs grew faster than PHEVs (146% vs 130%), and thus now represent 57.5% of all EV sales in the region, slightly below the 58% result for Q2.

Market share grew to 12%, up from 10.7% in Q2 and 5.5% in Q3 last year, a pretty strong result. This brought market share above 10% through 2026, 5.9% BEV and 4.3% PHEV.

Latin America’s overall vehicle market is growing at rates of around 10% annually, but thanks to strong EV growth, combustion-based powertrains (HEV, MHEV and ICEV) are stagnant: they fell by 3.7% last quarter, and barely grew by 3.1% this one, meaning it’s very likely that we’re reaching “Peak Combustion” in Latin America and the market will only go down from 2027 onwards.

Sales wise, we have a pretty stable list, with Brazil being the unsurprising winner, followed by Mexico and Colombia, just as in Q2. Uruguay follows, but then we have a new rising star: Ecuador, which nearly reached 5,000 BEVs sold last quarter! Costa Rica, Chile, and Argentina close the top 8 and make up all the countries with over 1.000 quarterly BEV sales.

PHEV sales are much more varied, though. Brazil, of course, leads again, but following come Mexico and Argentina, with Colombia in 4th place and Ecuador in 5th.

Market share: again, we have a pretty constant list, with the first 8 positions unchanged. Uruguay, unsurprisingly, leads the region with a very strong 50.7% EV market share (45.7% BEV), followed by Costa Rica, Colombia, Ecuador, and Brazil. Notable mention to Paraguay, the sixth country in our region to reach 10% EV market share through a quarter, but in this case thanks to PHEVs.

This has been an eventful year due to Donald’s dubious defense decisions, but even as high oil prices have been driving massive EV adoption and/or massive ICEV crashes all over the world, I feel the situation in Latin America isn’t as clear cut.

In the US and Europe, fuel prices have gone up by around 40–50%, at least in the case of gasoline. Now, prices have gone up similarly in some countries in Latin America, including Panama, Paraguay and Perú, but in many others, like Brazil, Colombia and Mexico, it has barely moved, either because of significant subsidies — direct or indirect — to maintain prices, or because it was already expensive before the war and the government did not make any adjustments to its price.

One would think that the countries most affected by high fuel prices would be at the forefront of the EV transition, but no: the largest increases in EV sales only partially track gasoline prices. For example: Panama and Perú are still lagging behind the region despite having the highest increase in the region (>50% in both cases), whereas Colombia and Brazil are sprinting ahead despite relatively stable fuel prices. What gives?

The answer, I believe, is linked to EV prices and how they compare with their ICEV competition. Yes, I know the TCO is a very important metric, but evidence in our markets point to the sticker price being the main driver of adoption. The cases of Brazil and Colombia on one hand, and Peru and Panama on the other, exemplify this very well:

Brazil and Colombia have had pretty stable fuel prices, up around 10% or less since the war started, yet both have booming markets, up 160% and 190%, respectively, with EV market share getting close to 20%. Meanwhile, Panama and Peru have seen gasoline prices spike over 50% from their pre-war costs, yet the former only showed a meager 57% growth in September, and the latter, though clocking in at a more impressive 270% YoY, remains at a disappointing 1.3% market share.

The main difference has to do with the nature of the market. In Brazil and Colombia, combustion vehicles are relatively expensive — in no small part due to tariffs to protect local industry — and EVs have effectively reached price parity with their combustion counterparts. Meanwhile, in Panama and Per,u EVs are just as cheap if not cheaper than in Brazil or Colombia, but combustion cars are significantly cheaper, with some entry-level models at or below USD$10.,00. This makes customers hesitant to try the new technologies by the simple logic that even if the EV will save a lot of money in the long run, $6,000 will buy a lot of gas.

And in Peru, this is particularly relevant insofar as gas-mobiles from Chinese brands are very popular. Changan, Jetour, Geely, and JAC together account for 20% of the Peruvian market largely thanks to their hyper-affordable combustion cars, and since the Chinese gas-mobile market is collapsing, it’s likely these brands would rather keep it like this for a few more months so they can move some of the inventory they won’t be able to sell at home before betting it all on EVs.

In general, this tracks for most markets, where EV prices compared to traditional combustion cars are probably the most relevant factor to keep track of. And of course, fuel prices do have an influence, but it seems it’s rather limited compared to, for example, what we’re seeing in Europe.

But the lessons are clear: Want to improve EV sales? Either make them cheaper, or make gas-mobiles more expensive.

At the beginning of this year, I was hopeful we would see 10% market share in the fourth quarter, normally the highest in the year. We went past that in Q2, and at 12%, we’re sitting far beyond what I considered feasible only 10 months ago.

Yet, there’s still a long path ahead. Aside from the 5 leading markets, we’re yet to see a country getting consistent BEV market share above 5%, and several large markets — Chile, Argentina, and Mexico — are still lagging. PHEVs have raised the bar in several countries, Paraguay most important of all, but we would rather see more BEVs.

This is not to be overtly pessimistic. The results so far are great, much better than we would’ve expected even a year ago. But as things improve, the bar rises, and I’m now waiting to see the moment when we will see 20% market share (Q3 2027?) or when e-truck sales will start to ramp up (2027? 2028?).

Passionate for all things Latin American, I’ve been closely following the energy and mobility transitions since they started to become present south of the equator.

Juan Diego Celemín Mojica has 104 posts and counting. See all posts by Juan Diego Celemín Mojica

Xenoblade Chronicles Studio Monolith Soft Helped Out With The Zelda: Ocarina Of Time Remake

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Nintendo confirmed the main devs earlier this week

Earlier this week, Nintendo confirmed Ocarina of Time for the Switch 2 was developed internally by Nintendo's Entertainment Planning & Development (EPD) Production Group No. 3.

There's been plenty of speculation about who else might be involved in the project, and Xenoblade Chronicles' developer Monolith Soft has now officially announced it assisted with certain aspects of the game, including the "design, programming and graphics".

Here's a rough translation, and you can see the game has now been officially added to Monolith's game page on its website:

Monolith Soft announcement: The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, for which Monolith Soft handled partial contract development of the game design, programming, and graphics, has been released by Nintendo Co., Ltd.

Monolith Soft has already played a huge part in Nintendo's releases this year (and since the arrival of the Switch 2 last June). Apart from the Switch 2 Edition of the Xenoblade games, it's helped out Splatoon Raiders this year. Xenoblade Chronicles 3 is also returning next month.

Other Zelda projects it's assisted with since the Switch generation include Tears of the Kingdom and Breath of the Wild.

Elon Musk Threw Away $474 Million (Or More) … But May Benefit From Failing

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Some people think Elon Musk has lost his mind. Others think he’s always been a charlatan and has just had an insane amount of luck. Some people think he’s a genius and that everything he touches will turn to gold. And then there’s also a decent portion of folks who think he’s an absolute idiot about some things but brilliant — or at least very clever — when it comes to others. Whatever you think of Elon Musk, though, there’s no denying he just threw away about half a billion dollars.

Musk reportedly spent at least $474 million on the midterm elections (because one individual is allowed to do that, apparently), and yet his picks — the key people he supported — got completely crushed. The politicians he spent all of that money to get elected did not get elected, and were actually not even close to getting elected. Apparently, voters in their jurisdictions thought they were horrible choices, sometimes historically so. Imagine if all of that money he spent had been spent on something good and useful instead! (Perhaps saving the lives of starving, poverty-stricken children and babies around the world? Perhaps stopping the spread of horrible diseases, since the US government has dropped such efforts on several threats? Perhaps to provide subsidies for electric vehicles, like Tesla sells, since Republicans in Congress and Donald Trump killed those? Perhaps to use solar power instead of fossil fuels to power AI data centers?)

Why did Elon Musk spend a whopping half a billion dollars (or more) on the 2026 midterm elections?

I know there are some people who don’t see this, but it’s been clear for a while that the following things are true about Elon Musk:

Musk largely targeted Texas with his political spending, and Texas had an epic, historic blue wave. Something that has been dreamt about for decades came true in dramatic fashion despite Elon Musk spending hundreds of millions of dollars to try to stop it. Ironically, Democratic politicians are the ones who support electric vehicles and solar power, key segments of Musk’s empire. It was California, led by Democrats, that birthed Tesla Motors and enabled it to grow and develop into an industrial behemoth. It was US policies under Democratic presidents and Congresses that also provided critical help for years upon years. So, despite Elon Musk’s worst instincts and biggest efforts politically, will his businesses actually do better under the newly elected Democratic leadership? Perhaps there are some corporate issues that need reigned in or need better regulation, and perhaps people Musk brazenly and often quite rudely campaigned against will squeeze down extra hard on his corporate actions, but policymakers who support cleantech over fossil fuels should, overall, mean more good news than bad news for Tesla and its broader mission, right?

Sure, Musk threw away $474 million and lost bigly, but is he going to be better off from failing in his efforts to pick the people running his current home state and representing it in the US government? What are the actual risks he and his companies face from the people he heavily campaigned against winning their elections? What are the benefits he and Tesla will get from fossil-loving, pollution-peddling Republican politicians no longer running the show in Texas and in Congress? One has to wonder.

By the way, one of the key people Musk campaigned against, James Talarico, has a message specifically for him. “Texas is not for sale,” Talarico said. Musk spent at least $150 million to try to get Talarico’s opponent elected. “The people of Texas delivered a crystal-clear message to the world’s first trillionaire: Texas is not for sale,” Talarico added. “The power in this state, in this country, belongs to the people, and we’ve got to make sure it stays that way.” What do the people want? Well, the people want clean air, clean and cheap energy, and less money in politics. Shouldn’t all of those things help Musk? On that last one, after all, if he wasn’t allowed to pour so much money into politics, he wouldn’t have thrown away half a billion dollars.

Zach is tryin' to help society help itself one word at a time. He spends most of his time here on CleanTechnica as its editor-in-chief and CEO. Zach is recognized globally as an electric vehicle, solar energy, and energy storage expert. He has presented about electric vehicles and renewable energy at conferences in India, the UAE, Ukraine, Poland, Germany, the Netherlands, the USA, Canada, and Curaçao.

‘Lawyer up’: Democrats target Trump, Big Tech after midterm sweep

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Democrats wasted little time after their midterm victory, warning tech CEOs to prepare for subpoenas and signaling fresh investigations into Trump’s business dealings.

Democratic lawmakers said Big Tech executives should expect a wave of subpoenas, along with investigations into US President Donald Trump’s business circle when the new Congress takes office in January.

As Democrats secured control of Congress in a sweeping victory on Tuesday, Democratic lawmakers were already firing warning shots at the Trump family and tech executives close to the US president.

“They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we’ll be printing,” said US Senator Ed Markey, who sits on the Senate Commerce Committee, referring to Big Tech executives.

US Representative Robert Garcia, the expected incoming House Oversight Committee chairman, said he plans to take on “the Trump Crime Family, corporate corruption, DHS and ICE abuses, and the cover-up of the Epstein files,” adding a two-word warning: “Lawyer up.”

The warnings follow a mid-term election campaign in which Democrats challenged Big Tech’s growing political influence and the rapid expansion of AI, raising concerns about data centers’ demands on electricity, safeguards for AI products and tech executives’ ties to Trump.

Punchbowl News reported on Wednesday that technology executives close to Trump, including Elon Musk, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, were expected to face scrutiny from House Oversight, Energy and Commerce and Senate Commerce.

The outlet also identified OpenAI CEO Sam Altman and Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei as potential witnesses facing questions about their companies’ AI products.

Trump’s crypto dealings also featured in mid-term Democratic campaign attacks, with candidates including Virginia Senator Mark Warner highlighting his memecoin earnings as part of a broader pattern of alleged corruption and self-enrichment.

“The amount of grift and corruption is unprecedented in American politics,” said Garcia during a press conference on Wednesday.

“The president himself has a crypto scheme where he’s making billions of dollars. This family is billions richer as the American public struggles.”

“What is being watched closely now is the lame-duck period,” said Andreja Cobeljic, head of derivatives trading at AMINA Bank in market commentary sent to Cointelegraph.

“Democrats have already told the Trump family and Republican leadership to lawyer up — a clear signal that corruption investigations are coming.”

House Judiciary Democrats have scrutinized Trump’s crypto businesses, publishing a November 2025 staff report alleging he used the presidency to enrich his family through crypto ventures tied to foreign interests while dismantling enforcement and anti-corruption safeguards.

House Judiciary Democrats have already started advertising positions for counsel and professional staff for the next Congress, including oversight and investigations roles.

Who Controls the Battery in Your Driveway?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Tesla brought home power backup to America. In Australia, an update is switching it off. North American owners just got Powershare with Powerwall. Australian owners of existing Teslas got a software update that appears to block the third-party gear they use to power their homes. It is a test case for who really controls the battery in your driveway.

Mark Purcell is an electrical engineer and energy advocate based in Noosa Heads, Queensland, Australia. He owns two Teslas and a bidirectional DC charger, and has been a member of the Australian Electric Vehicle Association since 2022. He has contributed content to CleanTechnica previously.

On 12 October, my Model Y ran my house. Through a Sigenergy bidirectional DC charger in my garage on Australia’s Sunshine Coast, it supplied about 25 kW to my home. The battery in my driveway holds several times the energy of a typical home battery, and I have already paid for it.

That may not last. Owners in several countries report that Tesla software from the 2026.38 branch, rolling out since October, cuts third-party discharge within minutes. On the Tesla Motors Club forum, owners describe power stopping after about three minutes with a battery management alert, or after 15 to 20 minutes whatever the load. The old Camp Mode and Service Mode workarounds no longer help. Drive Tesla Canada has covered the reports. EleJoy, Tlyard and Sigenergy users are all affected, and Tlyard now warns customers that Tesla’s 2026 software detects “simulated charging” and may cut the power link.

The release notes? “Security improvements” and minor fixes. Nothing about switching off a function owners use every day.

Here is the part that should interest readers everywhere. The same 2026.38 branch delivered Powershare with Powerwall home backup to owners in the US and Canada. That is a good thing. Tesla has finally joined Ford, GM, and Kia in letting the car back up the house.

Australia did not get it. Tesla Australia has said Powershare cannot be retrofitted to existing vehicles, and here Powershare is vehicle-to-load only. So, for every existing Tesla on Australian roads, third-party equipment is the only way to put the car to work for the home. If the software now blocks that equipment, Australian owners are left with nothing: not Tesla’s solution, and not anyone else’s.

The Tesla Australia owner’s manual says: “Do not use the Battery as a stationary power source. Doing so voids the warranty.” Tesla’s own Australian warranty page is narrower. It excludes damage caused by third-party adaptors or chargers, not the whole warranty.

Australian law is narrower again. Under the Australian Consumer Law, statutory consumer guarantees cannot be signed away by a line in a manual. Apple found this out when the Federal Court ordered A$9 million in penalties over the “Error 53” affair, after Apple told customers that third-party repairs had cost them their rights. American readers will recognise the shape of this argument from the right-to-repair fight.

Australia is a world leader in rooftop solar and is now subsidising home batteries at scale. The next step is obvious: the biggest battery most households will ever own is already parked outside. Bidirectional charging is how that capacity reaches the home and, in time, the grid.

That whole idea depends on interoperability. If one carmaker can switch off discharge overnight, through an over-the-air update the owner cannot roll back, and without saying so, then every bidirectional charger is a bet on that carmaker’s goodwill. Chargers bought in good faith become stranded. Competing suppliers lose access to the fleet. Grid operators lose flexibility they are counting on.

The ACCC, Australia’s competition and consumer regulator, has already named “denying interoperability” as conduct that can distort competition in digital platform markets. A car that decides which charger it will talk to is a platform in all but name.

I am deferring Tesla updates on both my cars, so I am also missing whatever genuine fixes they contain. I have asked Tesla in writing to hold updates until it confirms discharge will keep working. I have lodged a complaint with the ACCC under Australia’s competition and consumer laws. I have also asked the Australian Electric Vehicle Association, the national voice of EV users, to back it.

Tesla may have a real safety or engineering reason. If so, it should say what it is, and show why a less restrictive answer would not work: certified partner equipment, discharge limits, or a published interface that charger makers can build to.

Three things, and they apply well beyond Australia. Carmakers should disclose functional changes in plain language before they ship. Owners should be free to use the bidirectional charger of their choice. And regulators should set interoperability rules for vehicle discharge, as many already do for solar inverters. If we can make inverters speak a common language, we can stop cars locking out the chargers they plug into.

My car still powers my house. It does so only because I have stopped it updating. No owner, in any country, should have to choose between a current car and a working home battery.

“I am looking forward to the discussions around this topic,” David Waterworth, who helped get this article published, adds.

History Says This Is the Best Time of the Presidential Cycle To Invest. Could Wednesday's Rally Spark an Epic Bull Market?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Historically, stocks have soared in the third year of a presidential term.

Stocks soared on Wednesday as investors reacted to Democrats' sweeping win in the midterm elections.

The Dow Jones Industrial Average finished up 3.2%, while the S&P 500 (^GSPC +1.58%) gained 1.8%, and the Nasdaq Composite closed 1.4% higher.

The surge seemed to take Wall Street by surprise as stocks had initially fallen when results started to come in on fears of a data center backlash. However, by the time markets had opened on Wednesday, those fears seemed fully absorbed. While stocks with exposure to the AI infrastructure market fell on Wednesday, the rest of the market soared as investors cheered decisive results, President Trump's acceptance of his party's defeat, and a renewed potential for interest rates and inflation to come down, and for the war in Iran to end, bringing down oil prices.

Stocks tend to go up after an election as investors appreciate the degree of certainty that's been revealed, but Wednesday's gain was especially strong. Does it signal more to come? Historically, stocks perform particularly well during the third year of a presidential term.

Let's take a look at what history says about the current market cycle, and why it could be a great time to invest.

Historically, the third year of the four-year term is the strongest and most reliable period for the stock market. On average, the S&P 500 has gained 13.7% in the third year of the presidential cycle since 1980, and positive returns happen 78%-91% of the time, depending on the period that's cited.

According to the Presidential Election Cycle Theory, the midterm election often leads to economic stimulus as incumbent administrations typically look to improve the economy ahead of the next presidential election. Historically, the Federal Reserve has also been more accommodative during the third year, though that may happen in this cycle. Finally, Wall Street also prefers the sense of political certainty coming out of the midterm election.

From the week before the midterm to the end of the following year, a period of about 14 months, the S&P 500 has gained every time in the last 19 cycles, a remarkable streak, with an average gain of 20.3%.

In addition to the tailwind from this stage of the presidential cycle, stocks also look poised for further gains because investors, in particular, like a divided government, and with Democrats taking solid control of both houses of Congress and President Trump still in the White House, the government is clearly divided between both parties.

Wall Street prefers a divided government because it creates gridlock and makes it less likely that any extreme legislation passes. Investors also typically move back into stocks after the election as the risk of unsatisfying results has passed.

Finally, stocks tend to perform better from November through April than from May through October. There's no perfect explanation for that trend, but institutional investors tend to rebalance in the fourth quarter, corporate and holiday bonuses get put into the market, and tax-loss harvesting at the end of the year can also help. Alternatively, trading volumes tend to be lower in the summer when people go on vacations and aren't as plugged into the market.

Investors should keep in mind that these are just historical averages or trends, and that this historical pattern won't be able to overcome a black swan event or deeper, structural challenges, should they arrive.

However, the market's reaction on Wednesday bodes well for more gains. Though AI infrastructure stocks fell on worries about a data center slowdown, hyperscalers popped as investors bet that they would benefit from a slower AI expansion plan.

Nine out of 11 stock market sectors moved higher, with only energy and industrials falling. It's just one session, but stocks are off to a good start post-midterms, and the pieces are there for more gains.

Jeremy Bowman has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

Bay Area Air District Moves Forward with Nation’s Strongest Water Heater Standards

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Health advocates, environmental groups, heat pump installers, local electeds celebrate decision as.

SAN FRANCISCO — The Bay Area Air District voted today to move forward with the strongest clean appliance standards in the nation, putting millions of Bay Area homes on a path to switch from gas water heaters to zero-emission alternatives. Beginning in 2028, households will be required to install water heater appliances that meet clean air standards as old gas units burn out, with exemptions approved today for low-income households and complicated installations.

Gas appliances in Bay Area homes and buildings generate more smog-forming nitrogen oxide pollution than all of the region’s passenger cars combined. The Air District estimates its clean appliance standards for water heaters and furnaces could prevent up to 85 premature deaths and 15,000 asthma attacks each year.

The switch also saves families money. New research from RMI finds $360 in annual savings for Bay Area residents from switching from gas water heater to a heat pump. Analysis from SPUR projects $315 million in rebates for home energy efficiency will be available in the next three years to support the transition.

The vote follows thousands of public comments in support of the standards, despite an industry-backed opposition campaign linked to oil interests.

Supporters of the standards released the following statements:

“The research makes it clear that climate policies and affordability go hand-in-hand and today the Bay Area is taking the historic step of making clean home appliances the standard for a better future,” said Melissa Yu, Campaign Strategist for Clean Heat, Sierra Club. “A transition toward zero-emission gas heaters will reduce a key source of smog pollution, launch programs to help get more people access better technological solutions and lower energy costs. Today’s vote is a victory for cleaner air and for energy affordability.”

“Today’s vote will supercharge the job growth we’re already seeing at heat pump businesses like mine, opening new pathways for early-career Bay Area workers looking for family-sustaining wages and meaningful work. The Bay Area Air District showed real leadership in approving these standards. We’re proud to be on the front lines of equipping homes with energy-efficient technologies that cut energy bills, clean the air, and make homes more comfortable,” said Owen Krebs Grimsich, CEO and owner of 1-888 Heat Pumps.

“Today, the Bay Area chose clean air over fossil fuel pollution. Under these standards, when a household’s gas water heater wears out, it will be replaced with cleaner, more efficient technology like a heat pump, so millions of residents will breathe easier and save money on their energy bills. The Bay Area Air District showed real leadership, and the rest of the country should follow its lead,” said Tony Sirna, Deputy Policy Director for Buildings at Evergreen Action.

“Today’s vote is a big win for clean air, lower utility bills, and climate leadership,” said Berkeley City Council member Igor Tregub. “The Bay Area is moving forward with the nation’s strongest water heater standards, and millions of families here will breathe cleaner air as a result. These standards build in the flexibility we need to make the transition workable, while growing the market for clean technologies that lower bills and create good jobs.”

“Today, the Bay Area Air District said yes to saving lives and no to gas industry attempts to slow progress to clean our air,” said Fernando Gaytan, senior attorney on Earthjustice’s Right To Zero campaign. “The Bay Area is now one step closer to making non-polluting appliances the  norm thanks to the leadership of board members and staff. This policy will show the rest of the country what’s possible.”

“Today’s vote is a step forward for cleaner air, healthier homes, and a more affordable energy future. By advancing these standards, the Air District shows we can move away from polluting gas appliances without leaving families behind,” said Sam Fishman, sustainability and resilience policy manager, SPUR. “Bay Area cities, utilities, and regional programs are taking the important steps to invest more than $120 million annually in home electrification and energy-efficiency incentives. With these tools, we can make our homes safer for our planet and our health, too.”

“As regional leaders in building electrification, Acterra sees this vote as a vital win for local communities and public health,” said Lauren Weston, Executive Director at Acterra: Action for a Healthy Planet. “Transitioning to zero-emission heat pump water heaters clears toxic nitrogen oxide pollution from our neighborhoods while lowering utility bills and accelerating equitable access to clean, energy-efficient technology for all Bay Area households.”

“The Air District Board’s vote today to support the implementation of Rule 9-6 is a watershed victory for healthy and safe communities, moving from our dependence on fossil fuels to a cleaner, greener source of energy,” said Leslie Alden, Executive Director of Act Now Bay Area. “The Air District staff, the experts, the working groups, and so many dedicated members representing the Bay Area worked tirelessly from 2023 show once again that the Bay Area leads the way.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

McDonald's: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The margin story rests on a 98% franchised target, and whether baseline traffic can carry that model through 2028 is still open.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 4, 2026, McDonald's (MCD +6.53%) announced global comparable sales growth of 1.5% for the quarter. For long-term investors, customer visit frequency and menu pricing power remain critical factors for sustainable profit growth. Three things management said on the call stand out.

Management is testing new everyday affordable meal options across 40% of U.S. restaurants to address entry-level pricing. Fixing baseline guest traffic remains key for franchise profitability and store volume.

Specialty beverages are expanding across global markets to capture non-peak customer traffic. Orders containing these beverages generate check totals that are larger than the full-day average check.

Refranchising efforts in international operated markets (IOM, company-operated international stores) continue as the business aims for a 98% franchised portfolio by 2028. This asset-light model protects operating margins and capital efficiency.

Baseline guest traffic is turning positive heading into the fourth quarter following targeted promotional interventions. Management is watching pricing discipline and store execution across international markets.

The company maintains a vast global store footprint, strong brand equity, and unmatched supply chain scale. Future value depends on executing its multi-year growth plan, McDonald's > NEXT, through digital engagement and modern store remodels.
